s^{\alpha}=(a^{\alpha}_{i})_{i<\operatorname{lh}(s^{\alpha})}\in\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{<{\sf On}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}A^{{\kern-16.07475pt{<{\sf On}}\kern 6.01392pt}}_{{\kern-37.96413pt\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 6.01392pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{<{\sf On}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}A^{{\kern-16.07475pt{<{\sf On}}\kern 6.01392pt}}_{{\kern-37.96413pt\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 6.01392pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{<{\sf On}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}A^{{\kern-10.89307pt{<{\sf On}}\kern 3.675pt}}_{{\kern-26.52832pt\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.675pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{<{\sf On}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}A^{{\kern-9.84306pt{<{\sf On}}\kern 2.625pt}}_{{\kern-25.47832pt\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.625pt}}}
, if
\beta
is limit, define

\Conc(\bar{s})=\Conc_{\alpha<\beta}{s}^{\alpha}=\bigcup_{\varepsilon<\beta}\Conc_{\alpha<\varepsilon}{s}^{\alpha}=\bigcup_{\varepsilon<\beta}\Conc(\bar{s}\restriction\varepsilon),

while if
\beta=\gamma+1
, define

\Conc(\bar{s})=\Conc_{\alpha<\beta}{s}^{\alpha}=(\Conc_{\alpha<\gamma}{s}^{\alpha})\conc s^{\gamma}=\Conc(\bar{s}\restriction\gamma)\conc s^{\gamma}.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
\mathbb{S}=(S,0,+,\leq)
be a positively totally ordered monoid.
An
infinitary operation
or
infinitary sum
is a partial function
\Sum:\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{<{\sf On}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathbb{S}^{{\kern-14.47752pt{<{\sf On}}\kern 4.41669pt}}_{{\kern-36.3669pt\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.41669pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{<{\sf On}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathbb{S}^{{\kern-14.47752pt{<{\sf On}}\kern 4.41669pt}}_{{\kern-36.3669pt\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.41669pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{<{\sf On}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathbb{S}^{{\kern-9.94029pt{<{\sf On}}\kern 2.72223pt}}_{{\kern-25.57555pt\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.72223pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{<{\sf On}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathbb{S}^{{\kern-9.1625pt{<{\sf On}}\kern 1.94444pt}}_{{\kern-24.79776pt\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 1.94444pt}}}\to\mathbb{S}
satisfying:

(S1)

\Sum
is
compatible with

+
, i.e.,

•

\Sum(\emptyset)=0
,

•

\Sum(s)=s_{0}+...+s_{n}
for every non-empty finite
s=(s_{i})_{i\leq n}\in\dom(\Sum)
,

(S2)

\Sum
is
continuous
, i.e., for all
s\in\dom(\Sum)
such that
\operatorname{lh}(s)
is limit, if
s\restriction\alpha\in\dom(\Sum)
for cofinally many
\alpha<\operatorname{lh}(s)
, then

\Sum(s)=\sup\{\Sum(s\restriction\alpha)\mid\alpha<\operatorname{lh}(s),s\restriction\alpha\in\dom(\Sum)\}.

Furthermore, we say that
\Sum
is
natural
if additionally

(S3)

\dom(\Sum)
is
downward closed
, i.e., for all
s\in\dom(\Sum)
and
\alpha<\operatorname{lh}(s)
we have

s\restriction\alpha\in\dom(s),

(S4)

\Sum
is
associative
, i.e., for every
\bar{s}=(s^{\alpha})_{\alpha<\gamma}\in\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{<{\sf On}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\dom(\Sum)^{{\kern-48.2098pt{<{\sf On}}\kern 38.14896pt}}_{{\kern-70.09917pt\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 38.14896pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{<{\sf On}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\dom(\Sum)^{{\kern-48.2098pt{<{\sf On}}\kern 38.14896pt}}_{{\kern-70.09917pt\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 38.14896pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{<{\sf On}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\dom(\Sum)^{{\kern-39.00806pt{<{\sf On}}\kern 31.79pt}}_{{\kern-54.64331pt\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 31.79pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{<{\sf On}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\dom(\Sum)^{{\kern-35.30586pt{<{\sf On}}\kern 28.0878pt}}_{{\kern-50.94112pt\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 28.0878pt}}}
such that
\Conc(\bar{s})\in\dom(\Sum)
, we have

•

\langle\Sum(s^{\alpha})\mid\alpha<\gamma\rangle\in\dom(\Sum)
,

•

\Sum(\Conc(\bar{s}))=\Sum(\langle\Sum(s^{\alpha})\mid\alpha<\gamma\rangle)
,

(S5)

\Sum
is
commutative
, i.e., for every
s\in\dom(\Sum)
and any reordering
s^{\prime}
of
s
we have

•

s^{\prime}\in\dom(\Sum)
,

•

\Sum(s)=\Sum(s^{\prime})
.

In Definition
1.2
, we defined measures using infinitary sums of arbitrary form. One of the goals of this and the next section is to show that, even when a measure is defined using a non-natural sum, this sum must be natural on the subset of its domain where the measure is defined (Proposition
4.1
).

Every (positively totally ordered) monoid comes naturally equipped with an infinitary operation.

Definition 3.2
.

Let
\mathbb{S}=(S,0,+,\leq)
be a positively totally ordered monoid.
The partial function
\sum:\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{<{\sf On}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathbb{S}^{{\kern-14.47752pt{<{\sf On}}\kern 4.41669pt}}_{{\kern-36.3669pt\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.41669pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{<{\sf On}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathbb{S}^{{\kern-14.47752pt{<{\sf On}}\kern 4.41669pt}}_{{\kern-36.3669pt\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.41669pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{<{\sf On}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathbb{S}^{{\kern-9.94029pt{<{\sf On}}\kern 2.72223pt}}_{{\kern-25.57555pt\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.72223pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{<{\sf On}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathbb{S}^{{\kern-9.1625pt{<{\sf On}}\kern 1.94444pt}}_{{\kern-24.79776pt\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 1.94444pt}}}\to\mathbb{S}
is defined by

\sum(s)=\sup\{s_{\alpha_{0}}+...+s_{\alpha_{k}}\mid\alpha_{0}<...<\alpha_{k}<\gamma\}

for every ordinal
\gamma
and every sequence
s=\langle s_{\alpha}\mid\alpha<\gamma\rangle\in\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\gamma}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathbb{S}^{{\kern-7.06467pt{\gamma}\kern 4.41669pt}}_{{\kern-12.64577pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.41669pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\gamma}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathbb{S}^{{\kern-7.06467pt{\gamma}\kern 4.41669pt}}_{{\kern-12.64577pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.41669pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\gamma}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathbb{S}^{{\kern-4.6454pt{\gamma}\kern 2.72223pt}}_{{\kern-8.63191pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.72223pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\gamma}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathbb{S}^{{\kern-3.86761pt{\gamma}\kern 1.94444pt}}_{{\kern-7.85413pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.20052pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81206pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 1.94444pt}}}
such that the supremum
\sup\{s_{\alpha_{0}}+...+s_{\alpha_{k}}\mid\alpha_{0}<...<\alpha_{k}<\gamma\}
exists in
\mathbb{S}
.

Recall that a linear order is called
Dedekind-complete
if every set has a supremum and an infimum. Every linear order
({\mathbb{S}},\leq)
has a (unique, up to isomorphism) Dedekind-completion
(\hat{\mathbb{S}},\preceq)
in which it is dense (see, e.g.,
[
20
,
13
,
10
]
).

Given
A,B\subseteq\mathbb{S}
, we write
\sup(A)\leq\sup(B)
(even when
\sup(A)
or
\sup(B)
is not defined) if for every
c\in\mathbb{S}
, if
b\leq c
for every
b\in B
, then
a\leq c
for every
a\in A
.
It is easy to check that when both
\sup(A)
and
\sup(B)
are defined, this coincides with the usual
\leq
relationship of
\mathbb{S}
.

We also write
\sup(A)=\sup(B)
if both
\sup(A)\leq\sup(B)
and
\sup(A)\geq\sup(B)
, and similarly for other expressions like
\sup(A)\leq b
for
b\in\mathbb{S}
(using
b=\sup(\{b\})
), and so on.
With the same meaning, given
s,t\in\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{<{\sf On}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathbb{S}^{{\kern-14.47752pt{<{\sf On}}\kern 4.41669pt}}_{{\kern-36.3669pt\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.41669pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{<{\sf On}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathbb{S}^{{\kern-14.47752pt{<{\sf On}}\kern 4.41669pt}}_{{\kern-36.3669pt\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.41669pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{<{\sf On}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathbb{S}^{{\kern-9.94029pt{<{\sf On}}\kern 2.72223pt}}_{{\kern-25.57555pt\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.72223pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{<{\sf On}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathbb{S}^{{\kern-9.1625pt{<{\sf On}}\kern 1.94444pt}}_{{\kern-24.79776pt\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 1.94444pt}}}
we write
\sum(s)\leq\sum(t)
,
\sum(s)=\sum(t)
,
\sum(s)\leq b
for some
b\in\mathbb{S}
, and so on, even when
\sum(s)
or
\sum(t)
is not defined.

Remark 3.3
.

This coincides with comparing
\sup
of sets and
\sum
of sequences in the Dedekind-completion
2
2

2

The Dedekind-completion
(\hat{\mathbb{S}},\leq)
is a total order, but this is sufficient to define
\sum
on all sequences of
\mathbb{S}
. We do not need to define
\sum
on sequences of
\hat{\mathbb{S}}
.

Recall that a monoid
\mathbb{S}
is
lower semi-continuous
if for every
A,B\subseteq\mathbb{S}
such that
a=\sup A
and
b=\sup B
exist, we have that
a+b=\sup\{x+y\mid x\in A,y\in B\}
(
[
13
, Chapter XI.7]
).

Proposition 3.4
.

For every positively totally ordered monoid
\mathbb{S}=(S,0,+,\leq)
, the operation
\sum
is an infinitary sum satisfying additionally:

(a)

0
is the
neutral element
of
\sum
, i.e.,
\sum(s)=\sum(s\restriction(\mathbb{S}\setminus\{0\}))
for every
s\in\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{<{\sf On}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathbb{S}^{{\kern-14.47752pt{<{\sf On}}\kern 4.41669pt}}_{{\kern-36.3669pt\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.41669pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{<{\sf On}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathbb{S}^{{\kern-14.47752pt{<{\sf On}}\kern 4.41669pt}}_{{\kern-36.3669pt\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.41669pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{<{\sf On}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathbb{S}^{{\kern-9.94029pt{<{\sf On}}\kern 2.72223pt}}_{{\kern-25.57555pt\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.72223pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{<{\sf On}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathbb{S}^{{\kern-9.1625pt{<{\sf On}}\kern 1.94444pt}}_{{\kern-24.79776pt\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 1.94444pt}}}
.

In particular,
s\in\dom(\sum)
if and only if
s\restriction(\mathbb{S}\setminus\{0\})\in\dom(\sum)
.

(b)

\sum
is
infinitary-translation invariant
, i.e.

\sum(s)\leq\sum(t)

for every
s=(s_{i})_{i<\gamma},t=(t_{i})_{i<\delta}\in\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{<{\sf On}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathbb{S}^{{\kern-14.47752pt{<{\sf On}}\kern 4.41669pt}}_{{\kern-36.3669pt\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.41669pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{<{\sf On}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathbb{S}^{{\kern-14.47752pt{<{\sf On}}\kern 4.41669pt}}_{{\kern-36.3669pt\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.41669pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{<{\sf On}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathbb{S}^{{\kern-9.94029pt{<{\sf On}}\kern 2.72223pt}}_{{\kern-25.57555pt\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.72223pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{<{\sf On}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathbb{S}^{{\kern-9.1625pt{<{\sf On}}\kern 1.94444pt}}_{{\kern-24.79776pt\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 1.94444pt}}}
such that
\gamma\leq\delta
and
s_{i}\leq t_{i}
for every
i<\gamma
.

(c)

If the order of
\mathbb{S}
is Dedekind-complete, then
\sum
is
total
, i.e., it is defined on every sequence of any length.

In particular, in this case
\sum
satisfies Axiom
(S3)
.

(d)

If the order of
\mathbb{S}
is Dedekind-complete and the operation
+
of
\mathbb{S}
is lower-semicontinuous, then
\sum
satisfies Axiom
(S4)
.

(e)

The operation
+
of
\mathbb{S}
is commutative if and only if
\sum
satisfies Axiom
(S5)
.

Proof.

First, let us prove that
\sum
is an infinitary operation. It is clear that
\sum
satisfies Axioms
(S1)
.

To see that
\sum
satisfies Axiom
(S2)
, suppose that
s\in\dom(\sum)
has limit length, and that there is a sequence
\langle\beta_{i}\mid i<\gamma\rangle
cofinal in
\operatorname{lh}(s)
such that
s\restriction\beta_{i}\in\dom(\sum)
for all
i<\gamma
.
For every
\alpha<\gamma
, let
i(\alpha)
be minimal such that
\alpha<\beta_{i(\alpha)}
.
Then, for each
\alpha_{0}<...<\alpha_{n}<\gamma
we have
s_{\alpha_{0}}+...+s_{\alpha_{n}}\leq\sum(s\restriction\beta_{i(\alpha_{n})})\leq\sum(s)
, by definition of
\sum
.
This shows that
\sup\{\sum(s\restriction\beta_{i})\mid i<\gamma\}
exists and

\sum(s)=\sup\{s_{\alpha_{0}}+...+s_{\alpha_{n}}\mid n<\omega\}\leq\sup\{\sum(s\restriction\beta_{i})\mid i<\gamma\}\leq\sum(s),

and Axiom
(S2)
is satisfied, as wanted.

That
\sum
is infinitary-translation invariant and that
0
is the neutral element follow from the definition. Thus statements
(a)
and
(b)
hold.
It is also immediate that if the order of
\mathbb{S}
is Dedekind-complete, then
\sum
is total, and that the operation
+
of
\mathbb{S}
is commutative iff
\sum
satisfies Axiom
(S5)
.
Thus statements
(c)
and
(e)
hold.

Finally, to prove statement
(d)
(and that
\sum
satisfies Axiom
(S4)
), assume
+
is lower semi-continuous and
\leq
is Dedekind-complete. Then, by previous point
\sum
is total.
Consider first
s,t\in\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{<{\sf On}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathbb{S}^{{\kern-14.47752pt{<{\sf On}}\kern 4.41669pt}}_{{\kern-36.3669pt\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.41669pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{<{\sf On}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathbb{S}^{{\kern-14.47752pt{<{\sf On}}\kern 4.41669pt}}_{{\kern-36.3669pt\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.41669pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{<{\sf On}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathbb{S}^{{\kern-9.94029pt{<{\sf On}}\kern 2.72223pt}}_{{\kern-25.57555pt\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.72223pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{<{\sf On}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathbb{S}^{{\kern-9.1625pt{<{\sf On}}\kern 1.94444pt}}_{{\kern-24.79776pt\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 1.94444pt}}}
. Then,

\begin{gathered}\sum s+\sum t=\\
=\sup\{s_{i_{0}}+...+s_{i_{k}}\mid i_{0}{<}...{<}i_{k}{<}\operatorname{lh}(s)\}+\sup\{t_{j_{0}}+...+t_{j_{k}}\mid j_{0}{<}...{<}j_{k}{<}\operatorname{lh}(t)\}=\\
=\sup\{s_{i_{0}}+...+s_{i_{k}}+t_{j_{0}}+...+t_{j_{k}}\mid i_{0}<...<i_{k}<\operatorname{lh}(s),j_{0}<...<j_{k}<\operatorname{lh}(t)\}=\\
=\sum(s\conc t)\end{gathered}

by definition of
\sum
and of lower semi-continuity. By induction, it is easy to see that
\sum(s^{0}\conc...\conc s^{k})=\sum s^{0}+...+\sum s^{k}
for all finite
\bar{s}=(s^{i})_{i\leq k}\in\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{<\omega}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[13.56401pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.56401pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.86113pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.90082pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\dom(\Sum)^{{\kern-45.1212pt{<\omega}\kern 38.14896pt}}_{{\kern-62.93785pt\mathchoice{\makebox[13.56401pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.56401pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.86113pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.90082pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 38.14896pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{<\omega}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[13.56401pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.56401pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.86113pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.90082pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\dom(\Sum)^{{\kern-45.1212pt{<\omega}\kern 38.14896pt}}_{{\kern-62.93785pt\mathchoice{\makebox[13.56401pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.56401pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.86113pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.90082pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 38.14896pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{<\omega}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[13.56401pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.56401pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.86113pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.90082pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\dom(\Sum)^{{\kern-36.80193pt{<\omega}\kern 31.79pt}}_{{\kern-49.52814pt\mathchoice{\makebox[13.56401pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.56401pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.86113pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.90082pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 31.79pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{<\omega}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[13.56401pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.56401pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.86113pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.90082pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\dom(\Sum)^{{\kern-33.09973pt{<\omega}\kern 28.0878pt}}_{{\kern-45.82594pt\mathchoice{\makebox[13.56401pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[13.56401pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.86113pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.90082pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 28.0878pt}}}
.

Now fix
\beta\geq\omega
.
Let
\bar{s}=\langle s^{\alpha}:\alpha<\beta\rangle
be such that
s^{\alpha}\in\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{<{\sf On}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathbb{S}^{{\kern-14.47752pt{<{\sf On}}\kern 4.41669pt}}_{{\kern-36.3669pt\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.41669pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{<{\sf On}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathbb{S}^{{\kern-14.47752pt{<{\sf On}}\kern 4.41669pt}}_{{\kern-36.3669pt\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.41669pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{<{\sf On}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathbb{S}^{{\kern-9.94029pt{<{\sf On}}\kern 2.72223pt}}_{{\kern-25.57555pt\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.72223pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{<{\sf On}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathbb{S}^{{\kern-9.1625pt{<{\sf On}}\kern 1.94444pt}}_{{\kern-24.79776pt\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 1.94444pt}}}
for every
\alpha<\beta
.
Notice that

\sum(\Conc(\bar{s}))\leq\sum(\langle\sum(s^{\alpha}):\alpha<\beta\rangle),

by definition of
\sum
.
On the other hand, we have

\sum(\langle\sum(s^{\alpha}):\alpha<\beta\rangle)=\sup\{\sum(s^{\alpha_{0}})+...+\sum(s^{\alpha_{n}}):\alpha_{0}<...<\alpha_{n}<\beta\},

and by previous argument for finite sequences, we get

\sum(s^{\alpha_{0}})+...+\sum(s^{\alpha_{n}})=\sum(s^{\alpha_{0}}\conc...\conc s^{\alpha_{n}})\leq\sum(\Conc(\bar{s})).

All together, these show that
\sum(\Conc(\bar{s}))=\sum(\langle\sum(s^{\alpha}):\alpha<\beta\rangle)
, as wanted.
∎

Corollary 3.5
.

For every Dedekind-complete, lower semi-continuous, commutative positively totally ordered monoid
\mathbb{S}=(S,0,+,\leq)
, the operation
\sum
is a natural infinitary sum.

In Proposition
3.10
, we show that there are Dedekind-complete, lower semi-continuous, commutative positively totally ordered monoids of any desired degree. In particular, this shows that there are positively totally ordered monoids of any degree admitting a natural infinitary sum.

The following proposition shows that all natural infinitary sums come from restrictions of
\sum
.

Proposition 3.6
.

Let
\mathbb{S}=(S,0,+,\leq)
be a positively totally ordered monoid with a natural infinitary sum
\Sum:\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{<{\sf On}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathbb{S}^{{\kern-14.47752pt{<{\sf On}}\kern 4.41669pt}}_{{\kern-36.3669pt\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.41669pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{<{\sf On}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathbb{S}^{{\kern-14.47752pt{<{\sf On}}\kern 4.41669pt}}_{{\kern-36.3669pt\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.41669pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{<{\sf On}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathbb{S}^{{\kern-9.94029pt{<{\sf On}}\kern 2.72223pt}}_{{\kern-25.57555pt\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.72223pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{<{\sf On}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathbb{S}^{{\kern-9.1625pt{<{\sf On}}\kern 1.94444pt}}_{{\kern-24.79776pt\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 1.94444pt}}}\to\mathbb{S}
.
Then, we have that
\dom(\Sum)\subseteq\dom(\sum)
and
\Sum(s)=\sum(s)
for every
s\in\dom(\Sum)
.

Proof.

Assume
\Sum:\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{<{\sf On}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathbb{S}^{{\kern-14.47752pt{<{\sf On}}\kern 4.41669pt}}_{{\kern-36.3669pt\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.41669pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{<{\sf On}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathbb{S}^{{\kern-14.47752pt{<{\sf On}}\kern 4.41669pt}}_{{\kern-36.3669pt\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.41669pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{<{\sf On}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathbb{S}^{{\kern-9.94029pt{<{\sf On}}\kern 2.72223pt}}_{{\kern-25.57555pt\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.72223pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{<{\sf On}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathbb{S}^{{\kern-9.1625pt{<{\sf On}}\kern 1.94444pt}}_{{\kern-24.79776pt\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 1.94444pt}}}\to\mathbb{S}
is a natural infinitary sum.
Fix
s=(s_{\alpha})_{\alpha<\gamma}\in\dom(\Sum)
.
By induction on
\gamma
, we now show that
s\in\dom(\sum)
and

\Sum(s)=\sup\{s_{\alpha_{0}}+...+s_{\alpha_{k}}\mid\alpha_{0}<...<\alpha_{k}<\gamma\}=\sum(s),

as wanted.

It is clear that this is true if
\gamma
is finite, by Axiom
(S1)
.
So suppose
\gamma\geq\omega
.

Let
\alpha_{0}<...<\alpha_{k}<\gamma
, and let
t=(s_{\alpha_{i}})_{i<k}
. By Axiom
(S5)
, there is a reordering
s^{\prime}\in\dom(\Sum)
of
s
of limit length such that
t=s^{\prime}\restriction k
. This shows that
t\in\dom(\Sum)
, by Axiom
(S3)
, and consequently that
+
and
\sum
are commutative when restricted to
\{s_{\alpha}\mid\alpha<\operatorname{lh}(s)\}
, since
\Sum
satisfies Axiom
(S5)
.

Now if
\gamma=\beta+1
is infinite and the induction hypothesis holds for
1+\beta=\beta
, then

\Sum(s)=\Sum(s_{\beta}\conc(s\restriction\beta))=\sum(s_{\beta}\conc(s\restriction\beta))=\sum(s),

by Axiom
(S5)
, induction hypothesis, and commutativity of
\sum
on
\{s_{\alpha}\mid\alpha<\operatorname{lh}(s)\}
.

Suppose instead that
\gamma
is limit and the statement holds for all
\beta<\gamma
.
By Axioms
(S2)
and
(S3)
, we have
s\restriction\alpha\in\dom(\Sum)
for every
\alpha<\gamma
and

\Sum(s)=\sup\{\Sum(s\restriction\alpha)\mid\alpha<\gamma\}.

By induction hypothesis, we get
s\restriction\alpha\in\dom(\sum)
for every
\alpha<\gamma
and

\Sum(s)=\sup\{\sum(s\restriction\alpha)\mid\alpha<\gamma\}.

This shows that

\sup\{\sum(s\restriction\alpha)\mid\alpha<\gamma\}=\sup\{s_{\alpha_{0}}+...+s_{\alpha_{k}}\mid\alpha_{0}<...<\alpha_{k}<\gamma\}

is defined, and thus
s\in\dom(\sum)
and
\Sum(s)=\sup\{\sum(s\restriction\alpha)\mid\alpha<\gamma\}=\sum(s)
as wanted.
∎

In particular, by Proposition
3.6
we get the following.

Remark 3.7
.

Every natural infinitary sum satisfies the same properties of
\sum
(e.g., those of Proposition
3.4
), restricted to its domain.

3.1.
Examples of monoids

In the following, we provide different examples of positively totally ordered monoids. The first example is the well-known monoid
[0,\infty]
used in classical measure theory.

Example 3.8
.

We denote by
\mathbb{R}_{\infty}=([0,\infty],0,+,\leq)
the totally ordered monoid where
[0,\infty]\setminus\{\infty\}=[0,\infty)
is the positive cone of the real numbers, with usual order and operation, and
\infty
is the maximum of the order and an absorbing element for the operation (i.e.
a+\infty=\infty+a=\infty
for every
a\in[0,\infty]
).

Then,
\mathbb{R}_{\infty}
is an initially Archimedean, Dedekind-complete, lower semi-continuous,
0
-continuous, commutative, positively totally ordered monoid, thus
\sum
is a total natural infinitary sum on it, by Proposition
3.4
.

The operation
\sum
can be explicitly defined for any sequence
r=\langle r_{\beta}\mid\beta<\gamma\rangle
of elements of
[0,\infty]
in the following way:

•

\sum r=0
if
r
is empty or all elements of
r
are zero,

•

\sum r=r_{n_{1}}+...+r_{n_{k}}
if
r
does not contain
\infty
and has exactly
k
-many elements
\{r_{n_{1}},...,r_{n_{k}}\}
different from
0
,

•

\sum r=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}r_{n_{i}}
if
r
does not contain
\infty
and it has countably many elements that are different from
0
and
\{r_{n_{i}}\mid i<\omega\}
is an enumeration of them (by the absolute convergence theorem, the sum
\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}r_{n_{i}}
is independent of the chosen enumeration),

•

\sum r=\infty
otherwise.

Another class of examples is given by Dedekind-complete total orders.

Example 3.9
.

Let
(S,\leq)
be a Dedekind-complete linear order with a minimum
0
.
Then,
\mathbb{S}=(S,0,\max,\leq)
is a Dedekind-complete, lower semi-continuous, commutative, positively totally ordered monoid, and the infinitary operation
\sum=\sup
is a total natural infinitary sum.
If
\Deg(\mathbb{S})
is infinite, then
\mathbb{S}
is also
0
-continuous.

Notice that there are Dedekind-complete linear orders of any coinitiality (e.g.,
\kappa+1
with reverse order is a Dedekind-complete linear order of coinitiality
\kappa
for any cardinal
\kappa
). This shows that for any
\kappa
, there exists a Dedekind-complete, positively totally ordered commutative monoid
\mathbb{S}
of degree
\Deg(\mathbb{S})=\kappa
with a total natural infinitary sum.

Other, more interesting examples are given by the completion of groups.

Proposition 3.10
.

Let
\mathbb{G}=(G,0,+,\leq)
be a commutative densely totally ordered group. Then, the operation
+
and the order
\leq
can be extended to the Dedekind-completion
S_{\mathbb{G}}
of
\mathbb{G}
so that
\mathbb{S}_{\mathbb{G}}=(S_{\mathbb{G}},0,+,\leq)
is a Dedekind-complete, lower semi-continuous, totally ordered commutative monoid having
\mathbb{G}
as dense subgroup.

In particular,
\{0\}\cup\mathbb{S}_{\mathbb{G}}^{+}
is a Dedekind-complete, lower semi-continuous,
0
-continuous, positively totally ordered commutative monoid such that
\Deg(\mathbb{S}_{\mathbb{G}})=\Deg(\mathbb{G})
.

Proof.

Notice that every totally ordered group is a topological group (see, e.g., Theorem 10 and the following paragraph of
[
13
, Chapter II.8]
). In particular, this shows that the operation
+
is lower semi-continuous on
\mathbb{G}
.
By
[
10
]
, we can extend the operation
+
and the order
\leq
to
\mathbb{S}_{\mathbb{G}}
so that it is a Dedekind-complete, commutative totally ordered monoid having
\mathbb{G}
as dense subgroup, and the operation is lower semi-continuous by definition.
∎

In general the operation
\sum
need not be natural. The following example shows that
\sum
does not necessarily satisfy Axiom
(S3)
nor Axiom
(S4)
, even among lower semi-continuous, commutative, positively totally ordered monoids.

Example 3.11
.

Let
\mathbb{Q}_{\infty}=(\mathbb{Q}\cap[0,\infty))\cup\{\infty\}\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{\infty}
. Then,
\mathbb{Q}_{\infty}
is a lower semi-continuous, commutative, positively totally ordered submonoid of
\mathbb{R}_{\infty}
.
Let
(s_{n})_{n<\omega}
be a sequence of rational numbers summing up to
\pi
(or your favorite irrational number). Let also
t_{\gamma+n}=s_{n}
for every limit ordinal
\gamma
and natural number
n<\omega
.
Let
t=(t_{\alpha})_{\alpha<\omega^{2}}
.
Then, we have
\sum(t)=\infty
, however
t\restriction\alpha\notin\dom(\sum)
for every
\alpha<\operatorname{lh}(t)
.

The following examples show that
\sum
does not necessarily satisfy Axiom
(S4)
, even among Dedekind-complete, commutative, positively totally ordered monoids.

Example 3.12
.

Let
\mathbb{S}=([0,\infty)\cup\{\infty_{0},\infty_{1}\},0,+,\leq)
where
[0,\infty)
is the positive cone of the real numbers and
+
and
\leq
are its usual operation and order.
Define also
a<\infty_{0}<\infty_{1}
for all
a\in[0,\infty)
, and
a+\infty_{i}=\infty_{i}+a=\infty_{i}
for any
a\in[0,\infty)
, and
\infty_{i}+\infty_{j}=\infty_{1}
for every
i,j\in\{0,1\}
. Then,
\mathbb{S}
is a Dedekind-complete, commutative, positively totally ordered monoid, yet
\sum
is not associative, as

\sum(1^{(\omega)}\conc 1^{(\omega)})=\sum(1^{(\omega+\omega)})=\infty_{0}\neq\infty_{1}=\infty_{0}+\infty_{0}=\sum(1^{(\omega)})+\sum(1^{(\omega)}).

Example 3.13
.

Let
(\mathbb{M},0,+,\leq)
be a commutative totally ordered monoid without maximum.
Let
\mathbb{S}=\{0\}\times\mathbb{M}\cup\{1\}\times\mathbb{M}\sqcup\{\infty\}
, where the order is given by
(i,a)\leq(i,b)
for any
i\in\{0,1\}
and
a,b\in\mathbb{M}
satisfying
a\leq b
, and
(0,a)\leq(1,b)\leq\infty
for any
a,b\in\mathbb{M}
.
Let
\ast
be defined by
(i,a)\ast(j,b)=(i+j,a+b)
if
i+j\leq 1
,
(1,a)\ast(1,b)=\infty
, and
(i,a)\ast\infty=\infty\ast(i,a)=\infty
.
Then,
(\mathbb{S},(0,0),\ast,\leq)
is a commutative, positively totally ordered monoid, and it is Dedekind-complete if
\mathbb{M}
plus a maximum is. Yet,
\sum
is not associative, as if
\langle a_{i}\mid i<\gamma\rangle
is a cofinal sequence in
\mathbb{M}
, then

\sum(\langle(0,a_{i})\mid i<\gamma\rangle\conc\langle(0,a_{i})\mid i<\gamma\rangle)=(1,0),

\sum(\langle(0,a_{i})\mid i<\gamma\rangle)+\sum(\langle(0,a_{i})\mid i<\gamma\rangle)=(1,0)+(1,0)=\infty.

4.
Measures

For the classical theory of measures, we refer the reader to, e.g.,
[
17
,
24
]
.
Recall the definition of
\lambda^{+}
-measure, Definition
1.2
.
Notice that the notion of
\omega^{+}
-measure properly extends the notion of classical measure by allowing one to use monoids other than
\mathbb{R}_{\infty}
and measurable structures that are not
\sigma
-algebras.

Recall also that, adopting the notation of
[
17
, Exercise 17.4]
, given a weak
\lambda^{+}
-measure space
(X,\mathfrak{M},\mu)
, we say that
\mu
is continuous if all points are measurable of measure
\mu(x)=0
.

Even though the definition of a weak
\lambda^{+}
-measure can use any kind of infinitary sum
\Sum
, the next proposition shows that this
\Sum
must be natural at least on the range of
\mu
.
Since the values of
\Sum
outside the range of
\mu
do not matter in this context, Proposition
3.6
then implies that
\sum
is essentially the only infinitary sum that can be used to define measures.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
\mathbb{S}
be a positively totally ordered monoid with an infinitary operation
\Sum
, and let
(X,\mathfrak{M},\mu)
be a weak
\lambda^{+}
-measure space such that
\mu
takes values in
(\mathbb{S},\Sum)
. Let

\mathcal{D}=\{\langle\mu(A_{i})\mid i<\gamma\rangle\mid(A_{i})_{i<\gamma}\text{ is a family of disjoint sets of }\mathfrak{M}\text{ of length }\gamma<\lambda^{+}\}.

Then, we have that
\Sum\restriction\mathcal{D}
is natural, and thus

\mu(\bigcup_{i\in\gamma}A_{i})=\sum_{i\in\gamma}\mu(A_{i})

for every family
(A_{i})_{i<\gamma}
of disjoint sets of
\mathfrak{M}
of length
\gamma<\lambda^{+}
.

Proof.

We want to show that
\Sum\restriction\mathcal{D}
is natural, and the rest will follow from Proposition
3.6
.

First, it is clear that
\Sum\restriction\mathcal{D}
satisfies Axioms
(S1)
and
(S2)
since
\Sum
does, and it satisfies Axiom
(S3)
by definition of
\mathcal{D}
and by the first half of Axiom
(M4)
.
It is also immediate to check that it satisfies Axiom
(S5)
, by Axiom
(M4)
and since
\bigcup
is commutative.

Finally, for Axiom
(S4)
notice that if
\bar{s}=(s^{\alpha})_{\alpha<\gamma}\in\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{<{\sf On}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathcal{D}^{{\kern-16.0817pt{<{\sf On}}\kern 6.02086pt}}_{{\kern-37.97107pt\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 6.02086pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{<{\sf On}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathcal{D}^{{\kern-16.0817pt{<{\sf On}}\kern 6.02086pt}}_{{\kern-37.97107pt\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 6.02086pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{<{\sf On}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathcal{D}^{{\kern-10.96112pt{<{\sf On}}\kern 3.74306pt}}_{{\kern-26.59637pt\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.74306pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{<{\sf On}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\mathcal{D}^{{\kern-9.89168pt{<{\sf On}}\kern 2.67361pt}}_{{\kern-25.52693pt\mathchoice{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[15.01947pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[9.94972pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[7.10695pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.67361pt}}}
and
\Conc(\bar{s})\in\mathcal{D}
, and
\{A^{\alpha}_{i}\mid\alpha<\gamma,i<\operatorname{lh}(s^{\alpha})\}
are disjoint sets in
\mathfrak{M}
witnessing that
\Conc(\bar{s})\in\mathcal{D}
, then
\langle\bigcup_{i<\operatorname{lh}(s^{\alpha})}A^{\alpha}_{i}\mid\alpha<\gamma\rangle
is still a sequence of disjoint sets in
\mathfrak{M}
of length
\gamma<\lambda^{+}
. Thus,

\langle\Sum(s^{\alpha})\mid\alpha<\gamma\rangle=\langle\mu(\bigcup_{i<\operatorname{lh}(s^{\alpha})}A^{\alpha}_{i})\mid\alpha<\gamma\rangle\in\mathcal{D}

and

\displaystyle\Sum(\Conc(\bar{s}))

\displaystyle=\mu(\bigcup_{\alpha<\gamma}\bigcup_{i<\operatorname{lh}(s^{\alpha})}A^{\alpha}_{i})=

\displaystyle=\Sum(\langle\mu(\bigcup_{i<\operatorname{lh}(s^{\alpha})}A^{\alpha}_{i})\mid\alpha<\gamma\rangle)=

\displaystyle=\Sum(\langle\Sum(s^{\alpha})\mid\alpha<\gamma\rangle)

as wanted.
∎

Thus, without loss of generality,

for the rest of the paper we fix a positively totally ordered monoid
\mathbb{S}=(S,0,+,\leq)
and work with the infinitary sum
\Sum=\sum
from Definition
3.2
,

unless specified otherwise.
Furthermore, by restricting the domain of
\sum
to
\mathcal{D}
, in practice we can always assume that
\sum
is natural (on the relevant part of its domain).

4.1.
Properties of measures

Every classical measure is also countable subadditive, i.e.,
\mu(\bigcup\mathcal{V})\leq\sum_{V\in\mathcal{V}}\mu(V)
for every countable family of measurable sets
\mathcal{V}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
.
We can define an analogue property in this context.

Definition 4.2
.

A weak
\lambda^{+}
-measure space
(X,\mathfrak{M},\mu)
is
\lambda^{+}
-subadditive
if for every family
\mathcal{V}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
of size
|\mathcal{V}|\leq\lambda
, we have
\mu(\bigcup\mathcal{V})\leq\sum_{V\in\mathcal{V}}\mu(V)
.

In general, from the axioms of Definition
1.2
alone we cannot conclude that every weak
\lambda^{+}
-measure space
(X,\mathfrak{M},\mu)
is
\lambda^{+}
-subadditive.

Example 4.3
.

Let
\tau
be the usual euclidean topology on
\mathbb{R}
. Let
\mathbb{Q}+\pi=\{q+\pi\mid q\in\mathbb{Q}\}
, and let

\mathfrak{M}=\{O\cup D\mid O\in\tau,D\in\{\emptyset,\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{R}\setminus(\mathbb{Q}+\pi)\}\}.

Then, it is immediate to check that
\mathfrak{M}
is a weak
\omega^{+}
-measurable structure on
\mathbb{R}
.
Let
\mu_{L}:\mathfrak{M}_{L}\to\mathbb{R}_{\infty}
be the usual Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}
.
For every
M\in\mathfrak{M}
, define

\mu(M)=\mu_{L}(\mathrm{int}(M))
.

It is easy to check that
\mu
satisfies Axiom
(M1)
-
(M2)
, since
\mu_{L}
does. Also, for every
A,B\in\mathfrak{M}
such that
A\subseteq B
, we have
\mathrm{int}(A)\subseteq\mathrm{int}(B)
and
\mu(A)=\mu_{L}(\mathrm{int}(A))\leq\mu_{L}(\mathrm{int}(B))=\mu(B)
, thus Axiom
(M3)
holds as well.
Since no point is measurable in
\mathfrak{M}
, then
\mu
satisfies Axiom
(M5)
.
Finally, if
\mathcal{A}
is a family of disjoint measurable sets of size
\geq 2
, then
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\tau
, since
\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Q}
and
\mathbb{R}\setminus(\mathbb{Q}+\pi)
are dense in
\mathbb{R}
and have non-empty intersection. Thus
\mu
satisfies Axiom
(M4)
.
However,

\mu((\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Q})\cup(\mathbb{R}\setminus(\mathbb{Q}+\pi)))=\mu(\mathbb{R})=\infty\nleq 0=\mu(\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Q})+\mu(\mathbb{R}\setminus(\mathbb{Q}+\pi)),

thus
\mu
is not even finitely subadditive.

However, in every weak
\lambda^{+}
-measure space
(X,\mathfrak{M},\mu)
where
\mathfrak{M}
is sufficiently nice, the
\lambda^{+}
-measure
\mu
is indeed subadditive.

Definition 4.4
.

Given a weak
\lambda^{+}
-measure space
(X,\mathfrak{M},\mu)
, we say that a family
\mathcal{V}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
is
\lambda^{+}
-partitionable
if there exists a partition
\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
of
\bigcup\mathcal{V}
of size less than
\lambda^{+}
refining
\mathcal{V}
.

We say that
(X,\mathfrak{M},\mu)
is
\lambda^{+}
-partitionable
if every family
\mathcal{V}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
of size less than
\lambda^{+}
is
\lambda^{+}
-partitionable.

Every weak
\lambda^{+}
-measure space
(X,\mathfrak{M},\mu)
where
\mathfrak{M}
is closed under complements (and thus, a
\lambda^{+}
-algebra) is also
\lambda^{+}
-partitionable. However, these are not the only cases of
\lambda^{+}
-partitionable, weak
\lambda^{+}
-measure spaces (see Proposition
4.15
).

In these spaces, subadditivity holds in the expected way.

Proposition 4.5
.

Let
(X,\mathfrak{M},\mu)
be a weak
\lambda^{+}
-measure space. Then, for every
\lambda^{+}
-partitionable family
\mathcal{V}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
we have
\mu(\bigcup\mathcal{V})\leq\sum_{V\in\mathcal{V}}\mu(V)
.

Recall that we follow the convention introduced in Remark
3.3
and preceding paragraph: the fact that we write
\mu(\bigcup\mathcal{V})\leq\sum_{V\in\mathcal{V}}\mu(V)
does not imply that
\sum_{V\in\mathcal{V}}\mu(V)
exists in
\mathbb{S}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
be a family of disjoint sets of size
<\lambda^{+}
refining
\mathcal{V}
such that
\bigcup\mathcal{P}=\bigcup\mathcal{V}
.
Since
\mathcal{P}
refines
\mathcal{V}
, for every
P\in\mathcal{P}
there is
V\in\mathcal{V}
such that
P\subseteq V
. Using the axiom of choice, we can find a partition
\{\mathcal{P}_{V}\mid V\in\mathcal{V}\}
of
\mathcal{P}
such that
\mathcal{P}=\bigcup_{V\in\mathcal{V}}\mathcal{P}_{V}
and
P\subseteq V
for every
P\in\mathcal{P}_{V}
.
Thus,
(\bigcup\mathcal{P}_{V})_{V\in\mathcal{V}}
is still a partition of
\bigcup\mathcal{V}
refining
\mathcal{V}
with
\leq\lambda
elements different from
\emptyset
, and each
\bigcup\mathcal{P}_{V}
is measurable since
\mathfrak{M}
is closed under unions of size
\leq\lambda
.
By Axiom
(M3)
, we get
\mu(\bigcup\mathcal{P}_{V})\leq\mu(V)
for every
V\in\mathcal{V}
, and so

\mu(\bigcup\mathcal{V})=\mu(\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}V\in\mathcal{V},\\
\mathcal{P}_{V}\neq\emptyset\end{subarray}}\bigcup\mathcal{P}_{V})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}V\in\mathcal{V},\\
\mathcal{P}_{V}\neq\emptyset\end{subarray}}\mu(\bigcup\mathcal{P}_{V})=\sum_{V\in\mathcal{V}}\mu(\bigcup\mathcal{P}_{V})\leq\sum_{V\in\mathcal{V}}\mu(V)

by Axiom
(M4)
and by Proposition
3.4
(using that
\sum
is infinitary-translation invariant and
0=\mu(\emptyset)
is the neutral element of
\sum
).
∎

Corollary 4.6
.

Let
(X,\mathfrak{M},\mu)
be a
\lambda^{+}
-partitionable, weak
\lambda^{+}
-measure space. Then,
\mu
is
\lambda^{+}
-subadditive.

Notice that by Fact
2.1
and by our assumption on the weight of
X
, every family of open sets is partitionable.

Remark 4.7
.

Let
(X,\mathfrak{M},\mu)
be a weak
\lambda^{+}
-measure space. Then every family of open sets is
\lambda^{+}
-partitionable.

Therefore, by this and Proposition
4.5
, we obtain the following.

Corollary 4.8
.

Let
(X,\mathfrak{M},\mu)
be a weak
\lambda^{+}
-measure space. Then, the family of measure zero open sets
\mathcal{N}=\{V\in\tau\mid\mu(V)=0\}
is closed under unions of any size.

Another important property of classical measure spaces is that adding a set of measure zero should not increase the measure of a set.
Here, we get something similar under the additional assumption that the weak
\lambda^{+}
-measure space is
\lambda^{+}
-subadditive.

Definition 4.9
.

Let
(X,\mathfrak{M},\mu)
be a weak
\lambda^{+}
-measure space. A subset
N\subseteq X
is said to be
essentially null
if for every
M,M^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{M}
, we have that

M\setminus N=M^{\prime}\setminus N\qquad\text{ implies }\qquad\mu(M)=\mu(M^{\prime}).

Remark 4.10
.

Let
(X,\mathfrak{M},\mu)
be a weak
\lambda^{+}
-measure space. If
N\subseteq X
is essentially null and
N^{\prime}\subseteq N
, then
N^{\prime}
is essentially null as well, i.e., essentially null sets are closed under subsets.

Lemma 4.11
.

Let
(X,\mathfrak{M},\mu)
be a
\lambda^{+}
-subadditive, weak
\lambda^{+}
-measure space.
Then, every set of measure zero is also essentially null.

Proof.

Let
M,M^{\prime},N\in\mathfrak{M}
be such that
\mu(N)=0
and
M\setminus N=M^{\prime}\setminus N
.
Then
M\subseteq M^{\prime}\cup N
and
M^{\prime}\subseteq M\cup N
.
Thus, if
\mu
is
\lambda^{+}
-subadditive, we get

\mu(M)\leq\mu(M^{\prime}\cup N)\leq\mu(M^{\prime})+\mu(N)=\mu(M^{\prime})+0=\mu(M^{\prime})

and viceversa, therefore
\mu(M)=\mu(M^{\prime})
, as wanted.
∎

While these properties seem important enough that we might want to assume them, in practice we won’t need to: they are automatically satisfied in a substructure of any weak
\lambda^{+}
-measure space (see Proposition
4.15
).

4.2.
Minimal
\lambda^{+}
-measurable spaces

In order to prove our main impossibility theorem, we introduce a simple class of weakly
\lambda^{+}
-measurable spaces, to which we can always restrict when needed.

Definition 4.12
.

A
minimal
\lambda^{+}
-measurable space
is a weakly
\lambda^{+}
-measurable space
(X,\mathfrak{M})
such that for every
M\in\mathfrak{M}
there is a partition
M=D\cup O
satisfying that
|D|\leq\lambda
,
x\in\mathfrak{M}
for every
x\in D
, and
O\in\tau
is open.

A
minimal
\lambda^{+}
-measure space
is a weak
\lambda^{+}
-measure space
(X,\mathfrak{M},\mu)
where
(X,\mathfrak{M})
is a minimal
\lambda^{+}
-measurable space.

The term
minimal
comes from the fact that every weak
\lambda^{+}
-measure space contains a unique minimal
\lambda^{+}
-measure space that measures exactly the same points. This can be regarded as the
core
, or
kernel
, of the space.

Notice that given a weakly
\lambda^{+}
-measurable space
(X,\mathfrak{M})
, if
D
and
O
are subsets of
X
satisfying that
|D|\leq\lambda
,
x\in\mathfrak{M}
for every
x\in D
, and
O\in\tau
, then we get
D\in\mathfrak{M}
and
O\in\mathfrak{M}
, by definition of weakly
\lambda^{+}
-measurability, and therefore
D\cup O\in\mathfrak{M}
.
Thus, in a minimal
\lambda^{+}
-measurable space
(X,\mathfrak{M})

we have
M\in\mathfrak{M}
if and only if there is a partition
M=D\cup O
satisfying that
|D|\leq\lambda
,
x\in\mathfrak{M}
for every
x\in D
, and
O\in\tau
is open.

Given
A\subseteq X
, let
\mathfrak{M}^{\lambda}_{p}(A)
be the minimal family of subsets of
X
containing all open sets and all points of
A
, and closed under unions of size at most
\lambda
.
It is easy to check that
(X,\mathfrak{M}^{\lambda}_{p}(A))
is a minimal
\lambda^{+}
-measurable space, and that if
(X,\mathfrak{M})
is any other weakly
\lambda^{+}
-measurable space satisfying
A\subseteq\{x\in X\mid x\in\mathfrak{M}\}
, then necessarily
\mathfrak{M}^{\lambda}_{p}(A)\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
.
Therefore,
\mathfrak{M}^{\lambda}_{p}(A)
can be equivalently described as

\mathfrak{M}^{\lambda}_{p}(A):=\bigcap\{\mathfrak{M}:(X,\mathfrak{M})\text{ is a weakly }\lambda^{+}\text{-measurable space}\,\land\,x\in\mathfrak{M}\text{ for all }x\in A\}.

In particular, every minimal
\lambda^{+}
-measurable space
(X,\mathfrak{M})
is of the form
\mathfrak{M}=\mathfrak{M}^{\lambda}_{p}(A)
for
A=\{x\in X\mid x\in\mathfrak{M}\}
, and every weakly
\lambda^{+}
-measurable space
(X,\mathfrak{M})
contains a unique minimal
\lambda^{+}
-measurable subspace
(X,\mathfrak{M}^{\lambda}_{p}(A))
measuring the same points
A=\{x\in X\mid x\in\mathfrak{M}\}
of
\mathfrak{M}
.

Furthermore, if
(X,\mathfrak{M},\mu)
is a weak
\lambda^{+}
-measure space and
\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
is such that
(X,\mathfrak{M}^{\prime})
is weakly
\lambda^{+}
-measurable, then
(X,\mathfrak{M}^{\prime},\mu)
is also a weak
\lambda^{+}
-measure space.
This observation allows us to introduce the following definition.

Definition 4.13
.

The
kernel
of a weak
\lambda^{+}
-measure space
(X,\mathfrak{M},\mu)
is the minimal
\lambda^{+}
-measure space
\ker(X,\mathfrak{M},\mu)=(X,\ker(\mathfrak{M}),\mu)
, where

\ker(\mathfrak{M})=\mathfrak{M}^{\lambda}_{p}(\{x\in X\mid x\in\mathfrak{M}\})\subseteq\mathfrak{M}

is the unique minimal
\lambda^{+}
-measurable structure measuring the same points of
\mathfrak{M}
.

As a consequence, without loss of generality, we can restrict our study to minimal
\lambda^{+}
-measurable spaces: if we show that finding such a minimal structure is impossible, then we get automatically that finding anything else is impossible.

Minimal
\lambda^{+}
-measurable spaces have a nicer behavior than most weakly
\lambda^{+}
-measurable spaces, which makes them ideal candidates to work with.

Proposition 4.14
.

For every minimal
\lambda^{+}
-measurable space
(X,\mathfrak{M})
we have that
\mathfrak{M}
is closed under intersections of size less than
\kappa
.

Proof.

Recall that
X\subseteq\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\kappa}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\lambda^{{\kern-7.67737pt{\kappa}\kern 4.74307pt}}_{{\kern-13.88837pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.74307pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\kappa}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\lambda^{{\kern-7.67737pt{\kappa}\kern 4.74307pt}}_{{\kern-13.88837pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.74307pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\kappa}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\lambda^{{\kern-4.98601pt{\kappa}\kern 2.85834pt}}_{{\kern-9.42242pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.85834pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\kappa}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\lambda^{{\kern-4.16934pt{\kappa}\kern 2.04167pt}}_{{\kern-8.60576pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.04167pt}}}
. Let
(X,\mathfrak{M})
be a minimal
\lambda^{+}
-measurable space, and let
(M_{i})_{i<\gamma}
be a sequence of elements of
\mathfrak{M}
of length
\gamma<\kappa
. For every
i<\gamma
, let
D_{i},O_{i}\in\mathfrak{M}
be such that
M_{i}=D_{i}\cup O_{i}
,
|D_{i}|\leq\lambda
,
x\in\mathfrak{M}
for every
x\in D_{i}
, and
O_{i}\in\tau
.
Let
M=\bigcap_{i<\gamma}M_{i}
. Then, if
O=\bigcap_{i<\gamma}O_{i}
, we have that
O
is open, by
\kappa
-additivity of
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\kappa}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\lambda^{{\kern-7.67737pt{\kappa}\kern 4.74307pt}}_{{\kern-13.88837pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.74307pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\kappa}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\lambda^{{\kern-7.67737pt{\kappa}\kern 4.74307pt}}_{{\kern-13.88837pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.74307pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\kappa}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\lambda^{{\kern-4.98601pt{\kappa}\kern 2.85834pt}}_{{\kern-9.42242pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.85834pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\kappa}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\lambda^{{\kern-4.16934pt{\kappa}\kern 2.04167pt}}_{{\kern-8.60576pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.04167pt}}}
.
Also,

D=M\setminus O\subseteq\bigcup_{i<\gamma}D_{i}

has size
\leq\lambda
, since
\gamma<\kappa\leq\lambda
, and
x\in\mathfrak{M}
for every
x\in D
.
Thus
M=D\cup O\in\mathfrak{M}
as well, as wanted.
∎

In minimal
\lambda^{+}
-measure spaces, all families of measurable sets are essentially made of open sets, modulo a set of small size. By Fact
2.1
, we get then that they are
\lambda^{+}
-partitionable.

Proposition 4.15
.

Every minimal
\lambda^{+}
-measure space on
X
is
\lambda^{+}
-partitionable.

Proof.

Let
(X,\mathfrak{M},\mu)
be a minimal
\lambda^{+}
-measure space.
Consider
\mathcal{V}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
of size
\leq\lambda
.
Then, for each
M\in\mathcal{V}
we can find two disjoint sets
O(M)\in\tau
and
D(M)\in\mathfrak{M}
such that
M=O(M)\cup D(M)
,
|D(M)|\leq\lambda
, and
x\in\mathfrak{M}
for every
x\in D(M)
.

By Fact
2.1
, we can find a clopen partition
\mathcal{P}_{1}
of
O=\bigcup_{M\in\mathcal{V}}O(M)
refining
\{O(M)\mid M\in\mathcal{V}\}
(and thus
\mathcal{V}
).
Notice that
\mathcal{P}_{1}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
, since
\mathfrak{M}
contains all open subsets of
X
.
Also,
|\mathcal{P}_{1}|\leq\lambda
, since we assumed that
X
has weight
\leq\lambda
.

Let also
D=\bigcup\mathcal{V}\setminus O
, and
D^{\prime}=\bigcup_{M\in\mathcal{V}}D(M)
. Notice that
D\subseteq D^{\prime}
: this implies both that
|D|\leq\lambda
, and that
x\in\mathfrak{M}
for every
x\in D
. Therefore,
\mathcal{P}_{2}=\{\{x\}\mid x\in D\}
is a partition of
D
refining
\mathcal{V}
.

Thus,
\mathcal{P}_{1}\cup\mathcal{P}_{2}
is a partition of
\bigcup\mathcal{V}
refining
\mathcal{V}
, as wanted.
∎

By Corollary
4.6
, we get the following.

Corollary 4.16
.

Every minimal
\lambda^{+}
-measure space on
X
is
\lambda^{+}
-subadditive.

4.3.
Extensions and restrictions of measures

Given a weak
\lambda^{+}
-measure space
(Y,\mathfrak{M},\mu)
, we first define a way to induce a structure on a superspace
X\supseteq Y
.

Definition 4.17
.

Given two spaces
Y\subseteq X
and a weak
\lambda^{+}
-measure space
(Y,\mathfrak{M},\mu)
, define the set

\mathfrak{M}\uparrow X=\{A\subseteq X\mid A\cap Y\in\mathfrak{M}\},

and let

\mu\uparrow X:\mathfrak{M}\uparrow X\to\mathbb{S}

be the function given by

(\mu\uparrow X)(B)=\mu(B\cap Y)\text{ for all }B\in\mathfrak{M}\uparrow X.

Conversely, given a weak
\lambda^{+}
-measure space
(X,\mathfrak{M},\mu)
, we define the a way to induce a structure on a subspace
Y\subseteq X
.

Definition 4.18
.

Given two spaces
Y\subseteq X
and a weak
\lambda^{+}
-measure space
(X,\mathfrak{M},\mu)
, define

\mathfrak{M}\downarrow Y=\{M\cap Y\mid M\in\mathfrak{M}\}

and let
\mu\downarrow Y
be the partial function given by

(\mu\downarrow Y)(A)=\inf\{\mu(M)\mid M\in\mathfrak{M},M\cap Y=A\}

for all
A\in\mathfrak{M}\downarrow Y
such that the above
\inf
exists.

When we extend a measure from
Y
to
X
using
\uparrow
, the set
X\setminus Y
is always essentially null.
For these sets, the operations
\uparrow
and
\downarrow
are then the inverses of each other.

Lemma 4.19
.

Let
Z\subseteq Y\subseteq X
be sets and let
(Y,\mathfrak{M},\mu:\mathfrak{M}\to\mathbb{S})
be a weak
\lambda^{+}
-measure space such that
Z
is essentially null in it.
Then, we have

\mathfrak{M}=(\mathfrak{M}\uparrow X)\downarrow Y,\qquad\mu=(\mu\uparrow X)\downarrow Y,

and

\mathfrak{M}\subseteq(\mathfrak{M}\downarrow Z)\uparrow Y,\qquad\mu\subseteq(\mu\downarrow Z)\uparrow Y.

Proof.

The relations
\mathfrak{M}=(\mathfrak{M}\uparrow X)\downarrow Y
and
\mathfrak{M}\subseteq(\mathfrak{M}\downarrow Z)\uparrow Y
follow directly from the definition. For the equality
\mu=(\mu\uparrow X)\downarrow Y
it is enough to notice that

\displaystyle((\mu\uparrow X)\downarrow Y)(A)

\displaystyle=\inf\{(\mu\uparrow X)(M)\mid M\in\mathfrak{M}\uparrow X,M\cap Y=A\}=

\displaystyle=\inf\{\mu(A)\mid M\in\mathfrak{M}\uparrow X,M\cap Y=A\}=\mu(A)

for every
A\in\mathfrak{M}
(and in particular the
\inf
exists for every
A\in\mathfrak{M}
).
Similarly, for the inclusion
\mu\subseteq(\mu\downarrow Z)\uparrow Y
, the fact that
Z
is essentially null implies that
\mu(M)=\mu(A)
for every
A,M\in\mathfrak{M}
such that
M\cap Z=A\cap Z
, and thus

\displaystyle((\mu\downarrow Z)\uparrow Y)(A)=(\mu\downarrow Z)(A\cap Z)

\displaystyle=\inf\{\mu(M)\mid M\in\mathfrak{M},M\cap Z=A\cap Z\}=

\displaystyle=\inf\{\mu(A)\mid M\in\mathfrak{M},M\cap Z=A\cap Z\}=\mu(A)

for every
A\in\mathfrak{M}
, and in particular the
\inf
exists for every
A\in\mathfrak{M}
.
∎

The following lemmata give sufficient conditions for
(X,\mathfrak{M}\uparrow X,\mu\uparrow X)
or
(Y,\mathfrak{M}\downarrow Y,\mu\downarrow Y)
to be weak
\lambda^{+}
-measure spaces.

Lemma 4.20
.

For every topological space
X
and every weak
\lambda^{+}
-measure space
(Y,\mathfrak{M},\mu)
such that
Y\subseteq X
, we have that
(X,\mathfrak{M}\uparrow X)
is a weakly
\lambda^{+}
-measurable space, and
\mu\uparrow X
satisfies Axioms
(M1)
-
(M4)
.

Furthermore, if
Y
is closed in
X
, then
(X,\mathfrak{M}\uparrow X,\mu\uparrow X)
is a weak
\lambda^{+}
-measure space.

Proof.

Since
\mathfrak{M}
is closed under unions of size
\leq\lambda
, so is
\mathfrak{M}\uparrow X
. Besides,
\mathfrak{M}\uparrow X
contains all open sets of
X
, since the restriction of an open set of
X
to
Y
is still open in
Y
and
\mathfrak{M}
contains all open sets of
Y
.
It is also immediate to check that
\mu\uparrow X
satisfies Axioms
(M1)
-
(M4)
, since
\mu
satisfies them.

Now if
Y
is closed in
X
, then for every
x\in X
we have that either
x\in Y
and we are done because
\mu
satisfies Axiom
(M5)
; or
x\notin Y
and thus

(\mu\uparrow X)(x)=0=(\mu\uparrow X)(X\setminus Y),

which shows once again that
\mu\uparrow X
satisfies Axiom
(M5)
for
x
.
∎

Lemma 4.21
.

Let
(X,\mathfrak{M},\mu)
be a weak
\lambda^{+}
-measure space such that
\mathfrak{M}
is closed under finite intersections. Let
Y\in\mathfrak{M}
be a set of positive measure
\mu(Y)>0
.
Then,
(Y,\mathfrak{M}\downarrow Y,\mu\downarrow Y)
is a weak
\lambda^{+}
-measure space.

Proof.

Since
Y\in\mathfrak{M}
and
\mathfrak{M}
is closed under intersections, we get
M\cap Y\in\mathfrak{M}
for every
M\in\mathfrak{M}
, and so
\mathfrak{M}\downarrow Y=\{A\in\mathfrak{M}\mid A\subseteq Y\}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
.
Also,
\mu(M\cap Y)\leq\mu(M)
for every
M\in\mathfrak{M}
by Axiom
(M3)
, thus
\inf\{\mu(M)\mid M\in\mathfrak{M},M\cap Y=A\}=\mu(A)
exists for every
A\in\mathfrak{M}\downarrow Y
, and so
\mu\downarrow Y=\mu\restriction(\mathfrak{M}\downarrow Y)
. Therefore, it is immediate to check that
(Y,\mathfrak{M}\downarrow Y)
is a weakly
\lambda^{+}
-measurable space, since
(X,\mathfrak{M})
is, and that
\mu\downarrow Y
is a
\lambda^{+}
-measure on it, since
\mu
is.
∎

Lemma 4.22
.

Let
(X,\mathfrak{M},\mu)
be a
\lambda^{+}
-partitionable, weak
\lambda^{+}
-measure space, and let
Y\subseteq X
be such that
X\setminus Y
is essentially null.
Then,
\mathfrak{M}\downarrow Y\subseteq\dom(\mu\downarrow Y)
and
(Y,\mathfrak{M}\downarrow Y,\mu\downarrow Y)
satisfies Axioms
(M1)
-
(M4)
.

Proof.

By definition of essentially null set, we get that
\mu(M)=\mu(M^{\prime})
for all
M,M^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{M}
such that
M\cap Y=M^{\prime}\cap Y
, thus
\mu\downarrow Y
is defined on the whole
\mathfrak{M}\downarrow Y
.
It is then immediate to check that
(Y,\mathfrak{M}\downarrow Y)
is a weakly
\lambda^{+}
-measurable space, since
(X,\mathfrak{M})
is.
It is also easy to check that
\mu\downarrow Y
satisfies Axioms
(M1)
and
(M2)
, since
\mu
does.

Let
A,B\in\mathfrak{M}\downarrow Y
be such that
A\subseteq B
, and let
M_{A},M_{B}\in\mathfrak{M}
be such that
A=M_{A}\cap Y
and
B=M_{B}\cap Y
. Notice that
Y\cap(M_{A}\cup M_{B})=B
, and
M_{A}\cup M_{B}\in\mathfrak{M}
since
\mathfrak{M}
is closed under unions. Thus, we may assume
M_{A}\subseteq M_{B}
.
Then, by Axiom
(M3)
applied to
\mu
we have

(\mu\downarrow Y)(A)=\mu(M_{A})\leq\mu(M_{B})=(\mu\downarrow Y)(B)

and Axiom
(M3)
holds for
\mu\downarrow Y
as well.

Now let
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}\downarrow Y
be a family of disjoint measurable sets of size
|\mathcal{A}|<\lambda^{+}
. Let
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}=\{M_{A}\mid A\in\mathcal{A}\}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
be a family of measurable sets such that
M_{A}\cap Y=A
for every
A\in\mathcal{A}
. Since
(X,\mathfrak{M},\mu)
is
\lambda^{+}
-partitionable, there is a family of disjoint sets
\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
refining
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
and such that
\bigcup\mathcal{P}=\bigcup\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
. Since
\mathcal{P}
refines
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
, using the axiom of choice we can partition
\mathcal{P}
into families
\{\mathcal{P}_{A}\mid A\in\mathcal{A}\}
such that
P\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
implies
P\subseteq M_{A}
.
Notice that since
\mathcal{A}
is made of disjoint sets, we have that
P\cap A\neq\emptyset
implies
P\in\mathcal{P}_{A}
for every
P\in\mathcal{P}
.
Since
Y\cap\bigcup\mathcal{P}=\bigcup\mathcal{A}
, then we get
Y\cap\bigcup\mathcal{P}_{A}=A
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle(\mu\downarrow Y)(\bigcup\mathcal{A})

\displaystyle=\mu(\bigcup\mathcal{A}^{\prime})=\mu(\bigcup_{A\in\mathcal{A}}\bigcup\mathcal{P}_{A})=\sum_{A\in\mathcal{A}}\mu(\bigcup\mathcal{P}_{A})=

\displaystyle=\sum_{A\in\mathcal{A}}(\mu\downarrow Y)(\bigcup\mathcal{P}_{A}\cap Y)=\sum_{A\in\mathcal{A}}(\mu\downarrow Y)(A)

and Axiom
(M4)
is satisfied.
∎

Without additional assumptions, Axiom
(M5)
may fail in
(Y,\mathfrak{M}\downarrow Y,\mu\downarrow Y)
.
However, in practice, Axiom
(M5)
holds for all relevant pairs
Y\subseteq X
.
For instance, if every point that is measurable in
\mathfrak{M}\downarrow Y
is also measurable in
\mathfrak{M}
, the axiom follows directly from the definition of
\mu\downarrow Y
.
Similarly, if
\mathfrak{M}
is minimal, any point that is measurable in
\mathfrak{M}\downarrow Y
but not in
\mathfrak{M}
must be isolated, and hence satisfies Axiom
(M5)
trivially.
Altogether, we get the following.

Remark 4.23
.

Let
(X,\mathfrak{M},\mu)
be a weak
\lambda^{+}
-measure space, and let
Y\subseteq X
.
Assume that either
\mathfrak{M}
is minimal, or
\mathfrak{M}
measures all points of
Y
.
Then,
(Y,\mathfrak{M}\downarrow Y,\mu\downarrow Y)
satisfies Axiom
(M5)
.

By Corollary
4.6
and Lemma
4.11
, every measure zero set of a
\lambda^{+}
-partitionable space is essentially null. Thus, by
Lemma
4.22
and Remark
4.23
, we get the following.

Corollary 4.24
.

Let
(X,\mathfrak{M},\mu)
be a
\lambda^{+}
-partitionable, weak
\lambda^{+}
-measure space, and let
Y\subseteq X
be such that
X\setminus Y\in\mathfrak{M}
, and
\mu(X\setminus Y)=0
.
Assume that one of the following holds:

•

\mathfrak{M}
is minimal;

•

\mathfrak{M}
measures all points of
Y
.

Then,
(Y,\mathfrak{M}\downarrow Y,\mu\downarrow Y)
is a weak
\lambda^{+}
-measure space.

Recall that minimal
\lambda^{+}
-measure spaces are closed under finite intersections, by Proposition
4.14
, and are
\lambda^{+}
-partitionable, by Proposition
4.15
. Thus, by Lemma
4.21
and Corollary
4.24
, we get the following.

Corollary 4.25
.

Let
(X,\mathfrak{M},\mu)
be a minimal
\lambda^{+}
-measure space, and let
Y\subseteq X
be such that one of the following holds:

•

Y\in\mathfrak{M}
and
\mu(Y)>0
,

•

X\setminus Y\in\mathfrak{M}
and
\mu(X\setminus Y)=0
.

Then,
(Y,\mathfrak{M}\downarrow Y,\mu\downarrow Y)
is a minimal
\lambda^{+}
-measure space.

5.
On the existence of (trivial)
\lambda^{+}
-measures

In this section, we provide explicit examples of
\lambda^{+}
-measures on (subspaces of)
\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\kappa}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\lambda^{{\kern-7.67737pt{\kappa}\kern 4.74307pt}}_{{\kern-13.88837pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.74307pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\kappa}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\lambda^{{\kern-7.67737pt{\kappa}\kern 4.74307pt}}_{{\kern-13.88837pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.74307pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\kappa}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\lambda^{{\kern-4.98601pt{\kappa}\kern 2.85834pt}}_{{\kern-9.42242pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.85834pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\kappa}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\lambda^{{\kern-4.16934pt{\kappa}\kern 2.04167pt}}_{{\kern-8.60576pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.04167pt}}}
that are, in one way or another, not really satisfactory for the purposes of generalized descriptive set theory.
This will show the optimality of the assumptions in Theorem
1.3
.

5.1.
Dirac-like measures and the exceptionality of the countable case

In this subsection, we study measures with “small” support.

Recall that the support of a (classical) measure is the set of all points that do not have an open neighborhood of measure zero. A
Dirac measure
is a measure whose support has size
1
.

While useful in many applications, Dirac measures alone cannot produce a theory comparable, for example, to that of the Lebesgue measure on the reals. For this reason, when studying structural properties of Polish spaces, one usually regards all measures with finite support as trivial.

In analogy, in the generalized context we should view as trivial those
\lambda^{+}
-measures whose support has weight strictly less than
\lambda
. This can be formalized as follows.

Definition 5.1
.

Let
(X,\mathfrak{M},\mu)
be a weak
\lambda^{+}
-measure space. The
support
of
\mu
is the set

\operatorname{supp}(\mu)=X\setminus\bigcup\{O\mid O\text{ open},\mu(O)=0\}.

Definition 5.2
.

Let
\gamma
be a cardinal and let
(X,\mathfrak{M},\mu)
be a weak
\lambda^{+}
-measure space.
We say that
\mu
is
\gamma
-Dirac
if
\operatorname{supp}(\mu)
has weight strictly less than
\gamma
.

Since for finite (Hausdorff) spaces the weight and the size of the space coincide, it follows that, under this terminology, Dirac measures are exactly the
2
-Dirac ones, and
\omega
-Dirac measures are precisely the finite sums of Dirac measures.
On the other hand, the
\omega_{1}
-Dirac measures already include all classical measures on (separable) Polish spaces, and in particular they may be continuous and are not necessarily countable sums of Dirac measures.

Of course, we cannot rule out the existence of (
2
-)Dirac or
\omega
-Dirac
\lambda^{+}
-measures on
X\subseteq\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{\kappa}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\lambda^{{\kern-7.67737pt{\kappa}\kern 4.74307pt}}_{{\kern-13.88837pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.74307pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\kappa}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\lambda^{{\kern-7.67737pt{\kappa}\kern 4.74307pt}}_{{\kern-13.88837pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.74307pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\kappa}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\lambda^{{\kern-4.98601pt{\kappa}\kern 2.85834pt}}_{{\kern-9.42242pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.85834pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{\kappa}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\lambda^{{\kern-4.16934pt{\kappa}\kern 2.04167pt}}_{{\kern-8.60576pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.68175pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.82318pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.01656pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.04167pt}}}
; but any
\omega
-Dirac (
\lambda^{+}
-)measure is necessarily not continuous, so their existence does not contradict Theorem
1.3
.

However, unlike the classical case,
\omega
-Dirac measures may not be the only “trivial” (i.e.,
\lambda
-Dirac) measures on
